While anything runs the started action's button turns into the stop control — a black square inside a spinning ring — mapped by flags/phases (`layoutBusy`; `busy` + the last-used page-recognition button; `checkBusy` + `checkDecisionPhase`/`checkRecheckPhase`/`checkFixPhase`), so a run started elsewhere still shows its stop on the matching button; the model-download queue has no toolbar stop — it is cancelled where it starts. The range field is a two-way editor of the thumbnail selection, not a parallel scope: `PageListModel::selectedPagesRange()` formats the selection, `applyPagesRange()` replaces it (empty input = the whole document per the empty-selection convention, an input with no valid token changes nothing so a typo cannot silently widen the scope), and every pipeline action keeps reading the same selection through `scopePages()` — the per-button scope captions («все N» / «N стр.») are gone, the field answers "on which pages". In the manual pipeline «Проверить» always runs the decision pass without auto-fix (`checkScopeEnabledBlocks(FixSkip)`) — verify and fix are separate buttons; `check/autoFix` survives as a setting for the future automatic pipelines, which the «Авто» switch reserves the UX for (its button stays disabled until they land). Deleted: the ADR 167 preset stub («Ручной (Пошаговый)» + three grey presets — the «Авто» switch is its successor), the three-mode «Распознать ▾» picker (`recognizeMode` dissolved into explicit buttons), the «Проверить ▾» / «Экспорт ▾» menus (the export dialog already offers the scopes). Covered by `test_pagemodel::pageRangeFormat` / `pageRangeParse` / `pageRangeApply` (format, parse with clamping and garbage, the empty-input and round-trip contracts). | One glance at the toolbar answers "what is running and on which pages": the scope lives in one editable place instead of captions repeated on every button; verify/fix as separate buttons mirrors what the manual workflow actually is; and the groups are laid out for the automatic pipelines (mode switch + start button) without pretending they exist yet. |
| 172  | **Decision scheme v6: per-block prompts and thresholds, two questions in one `/v1/systemone` call, and a four-way routing that gates the fixer.** `roles.decision.blockPrompts[type]` may now ship as an **object** — `{statePrompt, question, questions}` (the string shape stays the fix/blockRecognition vocabulary; the parser branches on the value type) — where `statePrompt` is the block type's equivalence rules carried into `state.notation`, and `questions` is the ready dict passed verbatim to the request: `match` (noul) + a 4-class `diagnosis` choice. The draft's `has_text`/`readable` noul questions were cut before shipping: the llama-server slot log showed d3 answers **each question of a request with its own forward pass** (~1.4 s each — four questions made one block ~5.5 s; the joint single-pass reading is a clef-flash property, `archive/models/docs/17-d1-verification-study.md` §2 records the same for lfm2-d1), and the diagnosis classes `empty_crop`/`unreadable_or_clipped` already cover those routes. The parser and `DecisionResult::routedStatus()` keep supporting all five answers (`hasText`/`readable`, −1 = not asked; the choice map accepted under `probabilities` — the measured spelling, next to a string label — under `choice`, or bare): an empty crop (noul below `emptyThreshold` 0.25 or top = `empty_crop`) and an unreadable/clipped one (noul below `readableThreshold` 0.45 or top = `unreadable_or_clipped`) → the existing **Review** status — human eyes, never a fixer call, even under `check/autoFix` (only Mismatch feeds the fix queue); **Ok** requires both heads to agree (`p(match) ≥ threshold` ∧ top = `exact_match`); everything else is **Mismatch**. Without aux answers the two noul gates are simply skipped. Thresholds: `matchThresholdByBlock[alias]` → role `matchThreshold` → the `decision/matchThreshold` setting (0.25/0.45 code defaults for the two cut-offs); `d3-flash`/`d3-mini` ship the calibration and the two-question dict, and since 19 Oct clef-flash ships the **same scheme** — its joint question reading makes the second question free (the profile texts and the per-block map moved over from d3-mini, uncalibrated for clef; the measured 0.7 stays the role-level fallback). The fix profiles (`qwen3.5-4b/9b`, `gemma-4-e4b/12b`) carry the same equivalence rules per block type ("Equivalent (never fix) / Strictly different (fix)") so the two models cannot fight over е/ё, homoglyphs, LaTeX commands or table cells — synced in the JSONs, not injected across profiles in code. The block vocabulary gains the 19th type `key_value` (d3 ships its prompts and a 0.8 threshold). Tests: `test_decision_model` (questions passthrough, all choice spellings incl. the measured one, the four outcomes), `test_model_preset_catalog::decisionRoleShipsTheStructuredStateWording` (clef v5-empty vs d3 v6 invariants) + fix-sync anchors, `test_app_import::decisionV6RoutingSendsUnreadableCropsToReview`. | The decision-model call is the pipeline's hot path, and the draft's "four questions for free" premise measured false on d3 — two questions keep the design's substance (the noul/choice dual gate plus the empty/unreadable cut-offs through the diagnosis classes) at half the cost. The remaining noul gates stay implemented for a server that reads jointly. `docs/todo/decisions_improvements.md` is the original proposal. |
| 173  | **The decision confidence is persisted in `project.json`, turning threshold calibration into one run instead of an iteration per candidate threshold.** `BoundingBox::confidence`/`correctedConfidence` (the noul P(match) of the verdict, `verdictApplied` already stored it in memory) are now written to the box objects — `confidence` only when ≥ 0, so projects saved before any decision pass stay byte-stable — and read back with the −1 default; additive field, no format version bump (an older build ignores the key). The consumer is the eval stand `OCRtest/decision_models/test 2/evaluate.py`: since Ok = p ≥ t, it recomputes the verdict from the saved P(match) at any candidate threshold — raising t converts Ok blocks with p below it into flags (FN falls, FP rises), Review verdicts stay flags at every t — and prints the whole FN/FP(t) curve per model. Caveat: a Mismatch driven by the diagnosis head (fixable at high p) is invisible to the curve, so it overstates FN slightly; blocks saved without confidence sit out. Tests: `test_project_store::saveLoadRoundTrip` covers both layers' confidence round-trip and the −1 default for boxes saved without one. | The iteration-2 measurement left the residual FN as confidently-wrong errors (p above the profile thresholds) while FP sat at ~5–10 % — but confidence was a debug-only field, so every threshold hypothesis cost a full re-verification run through the app. With the numbers on disk the 19 Oct threshold raise (text 0.75, equation/formula/table 0.8, direction per the old v5 measurement T=0.8 → 13 % missed / 25 % false alarms) becomes one point on a curve the stand prints after a single run — and can be dialled to the FN≈0-at-some-FP goal without re-running the app. |
| 174  | **The preview overlay paints the page furniture grey: a fifth block kind, `Service`.** `BoxListModel::BoxKind` grows a `Service` value (4) and `kindFor()` returns it for the labels that carry no content — `header`, `footer`, `page_header`, `page_footer`, `page_number`, `seal` (the same set the verification window groups as «service»); `ImagePreview.qml` maps it to the new `Theme.overlayService*` triple (grey `#757575` / `#D9D9D9` / `#10757575`) instead of the green text hue. The kind is decided by the label only, so it does not depend on the model's style profile — a running head is furniture whichever vocabulary names it. The rest of the overlay (image / table / formula hues, the selected frame, the status dot) is unchanged, and the label→style map (ADR 88) is untouched — the Markdown rendering of a service block is still whatever the profile styles it as. | One hue for every text block made the running heads and the page numbers look like content: on a scanned page the green frame around «ИСПОЛЬЗУЕМЫЕ ОБОЗНАЧЕНИЯ» and the one around a stray page number carried the same weight, and the eye could not tell the document apart from its furniture. A separate kind is the smallest change that says so — a colour swap per style would have meant touching the shared label map and the Markdown output with it. |
| 175  | **The page preview zooms and pans, and it scrolls the selected block out from under the block panel.** `ImagePreview.qml` grows a zoom (0.25–8×, relative to the fit-to-window size — 100 % = the whole page in view), a pan offset and a `WheelHandler`: the wheel scrolls the page vertically (Shift — horizontally), Ctrl/⌘+wheel zooms around the cursor, dragging the page pans it (the drag delta is taken in the **viewport's** frame — the area's own frame moves with the page and would halve every step). The zoom controls live in the **page toolbar** (`PageToolBar`): a button showing the current scale opens the menu «Zoom in / Zoom out / Fit page» (Ctrl/⌘+`+`, `-`, `0`); the toolbar reaches the preview through the `preview` property that `ImagePanel` passes. The **overlay is drawn in screen pixels, not in page pixels** — the block frames, the status dots, the mini toolbar and the resize handles keep their size at any zoom, and the boxes are positioned by the painted rect (`paintedWidth × zoom`). The block panel floats over the preview's bottom edge: `ImagePanel` passes the overlap as `bottomInset`, the page is kept inside the free area above it, and when the selected block would end up under the panel the preview scrolls up until the block is clear (`ensureVisible()`, on the selection, the page and the inset). `OcrImageProvider` now serves the page at **twice** the requested size (bounded by the page's native resolution), so zooming in shows pixels instead of a blown-up preview. | The preview was a fixed fit-to-window image: a dense two-column page could not be read, and the block panel covered the very block the user had just clicked — the click landed on a box the panel then hid. Both are what every other reader does: a zoom/pan and a scroll-to-the-selection. The zoom controls belong to the page toolbar (the panel that owns the page), not floating over the page they change. The overlay stays in screen pixels (rather than scaling the whole overlay tree) so the chrome does not grow with the zoom, and the provider serves a double-resolution page so the zoom is worth using. |
| 176  | **The QML module is declared beside the `llocr` target and the QML tree moved `resources/qml` → `src/qml`; both Windows generators (NMake JOM kit and the new Ninja `dev-win` preset) build from scratch again.** The old layout — module declared in the root `CMakeLists.txt` for the target in `src/` (the ADR 101 amendment) — mis-registers the executable's metatypes chain per generator: Ninja emitted **no** producer edges for `llocr_json_file_list.txt` → `moc --collect-json` → `qt6llocr_metatypes.json` (the registrar consumed a stale 0-byte file), and the NMake/JOM makefiles carried the rules with an unsatisfiable ordering — `llocr_tooling`/`llocr_qmltyperegistration` read `src\llocr_autogen\timestamp` and the autogen `ParseCache.txt`, while `llocr_autogen_timestamp_deps` itself depends on `llocr_tooling`, so `add_dependencies(llocr_tooling llocr_autogen)` is refused as a cycle and every fresh build died with «dependent 'src\llocr_autogen\timestamp' does not exist» until `llocr_autogen` was bootstrapped by hand. Declaring the module in `src/` fixes the wiring (Ninja gains the edges), and moving the QML files under `src/qml/` removes the last Win32 blocker: qmlcachegen names its outputs `<target>_<path relative to the declaring directory>`, so from `src/` the old `resources/qml` location produced `llocr_../resources/…` — a trailing-dot component Win32 can neither create nor read (this is what finally broke the ADR 101 root-placement workaround from the other side). `CMakePresets.json` `dev-win` switched from `NMake Makefiles` to `Ninja` (the VC-bundled ninja, same MSVC 2022 compiler, same Qt, same vcpkg ZLIB) as the supported ready-made tree. Verified: from-scratch builds pass with **both** generators — `cmake --preset dev-win` + build produces `llocr.exe` and all tests with no manual steps, and the legacy JOM Release tree relinked `llocr` end-to-end after `cmake .`. | jom/nmake resolve a file dependency only within one makefile and check it before running any command, so no project-side ordering can repair the graph; moving to the canonical Qt layout (module beside target, QML beside module) is the only lever that removes the failure class instead of papering over it. Ninja keeps one global graph, where the same project always built. |
| 177  | **A block can be added by hand: the page toolbar's five type buttons and the type menu put an empty block of that type on the current page, and every block can be moved and resized.** `AppController::addBlock(type)` appends an empty `BoundingBox` (label = the canonical block type — `text`, `image`, `table`, `formula`, `code`, …, the same vocabulary the verification window uses) to the current page, selects it and marks the page edited; a page that had no result becomes markup-only («только разметка»), and the page text follows the blocks only while it still *is* the block-derived text (a manual edit or a raw page OCR keeps its own text, as `applyPageTextChanged` does). The type buttons in `PageToolBar.qml` (T / image / table / Σ / code) and the dropdown with every `BlockNames` type call it; `BlockNames.qml` carries the type id next to the translated name (`blockTypes` + `blockType(name)`), and the label map gained `formula → equation` so a formula block renders as a formula. The overlay interaction changed with it: the drag and the resize handles are no longer the exclusive right of an image block — **every** block can be moved and resized (`ImagePreview.qml`), because a block the user placed must be placeable (ADR 153's `isImageBox` rule keeps deciding the *delete* affordance). The text of a new block arrives the usual way: the per-block «Распознать» (block recognition) transcribes the region the user framed. Tests: `test_app_import::addedBlockLandsOnTheCurrentPage`. | The OCR misses a region and the user has to mark it themselves — the toolbar already had the buttons for it, but a block that cannot be moved is a decoration. The type vocabulary is the one the app already knows (the verification block types), so an added block is verified like any other and there is no second list to keep in sync. |
| 178  | **The automatic check and the automatic fix are gone: checking and fixing are two separate user actions.** The two settings switches of the verification window are **deleted** — `check/autoCheck` («Автоматическая проверка», run the decision pass after a recognition run) and `check/autoFix` («Автоматическое исправление после проверки», feed the rejected blocks to the fix model right after the check) — together with their whole code path: `SettingsStore` loses the `autoCheck`/`autoFix` properties, getters, setters, signals and default rows (the stored keys `check/autoCheck`, `check/autoFix` and the older `check/autoRecheck` are dropped at startup with the other retired keys); `AppController` loses the recognition-finished handler that started `checkAllEnabledBlocks()` (and with it the `m_recognitionStopped` flag that existed only to suppress that auto-check); `VerificationQueueController` loses the `FixMode` enum (`FixFromSetting`/`FixSkip`/`FixForce`), the `m_autoFix`/`m_runFix` members, the `m_fixQueue` and the decision-phase hand-over to the fix phase — a check run now ends after the decision pass, and the fix is a separate queue the «Fix» button starts over the problem blocks (`fixScopeProblemBlocks` / `fixAllProblemBlocks` / `fixSelectedBlock`). The `fixMode` parameter disappears from `AppController::checkEnabledBlocksOnPage` / `checkAllEnabledBlocks` / `checkScopeEnabledBlocks` and from the matching `VerificationQueueController` slots, so the QML type registration of `VerificationQueueController` (which existed only for the `FixMode` constants) is dropped from `main.cpp`. The verification window keeps «Показывать уверенность модели» and the block-type list; its help text now says that fixing is a separate step. Tests: `test_app_import::decisionMismatchIsFixedByTheSeparateFixRun` (the check leaves the mismatches marked; the separate fix corrects them), `::decisionMismatchStaysUnfixed`, `::confirmedVerdictLandsOnTheCorrectionLayer`; `test_settings_store::migrationDropsTheRetiredKeys` pins the dropped keys. Supersedes the ADR 171/166 «`check/autoFix` survives as a setting for the future automatic pipelines» — the automatic pipelines are not coming, so the setting only promised a behaviour that no code path used. | The two switches described a pipeline the app does not have: the «Авто» segmented switch in the header is still a disabled stub (ADR 171), so «Автоматическая проверка» promised a check the user could not get and «Автоматическое исправление после проверки» promised a fix nobody would see. A checkbox that toggles nothing is worse than no checkbox — the manual pipeline already has «Проверить» and «Исправить» as two buttons, and the ADR 171 rework made that split explicit. Removing the switches also removes the last user of the `FixMode` enum and of the `m_fixQueue` hand-over, so the check run costs one server role switch instead of two. |
| 179  | **The memory estimate tells the truth, and the d3-mini profile fits a 6 GB card.** The estimate (`ModelMemoryEstimator`) used to count the weights file and a KV cache computed from a guess: it did not know the `qwen35` architecture (the d3-mini base), so it fell back to a 28-layer llama shape; it derived the head dim as `n_embd / n_head` (160 instead of the real 256) and ignored the projector and the graph's working set — so it reported ≈3.5 GB for a server that really takes 5.2 GB of VRAM. It now reads the architecture the file declares, the true head dim (`attention.key_length`), the projector file and the working set the ubatch reserves, and it counts the **attention layers only**: a hybrid architecture (Qwen3.5) keeps the recurrent state in SSM tensors, and the tensor table says which layers carry K and V — the d3-mini keeps a KV cache in 8 of 32 layers, which is why its cache is 256 MiB and not 1 GiB. `RuntimeController::estimateModelMemory()` passes the task's projector and ubatch and reports the footprint against the **card's** VRAM (`HardwareInfo`), not only against the RAM; the Launch tab shows it (weights · projector · KV cache · working set vs the card's video memory) and warns when the model does not fit. The d3-mini profile pins `--batch-size`/`--ubatch-size` **6144** instead of 8192: the compute buffer is reserved for the biggest ubatch the server may see and scales with it (1312 MiB at 8192, 984 at 6144), so the shipped server drops from 5233 to 4905 MiB on a 6 GB card, with the longest prompt the corpus produces (a large table, ≈5.2k tokens) still fitting in one ubatch. Measured on a GTX 1660 Ti (llama.cpp b11707): the projector on the CPU (`--no-mmproj-offload`) costs three times the latency, `--cache-reuse`/`--cache-ram` do not engage for a decision model, and `--fit` shrinks the ubatch to 512 — which caps the vision budget at 256 image tokens and makes every decision prompt too long. Tests: `test_model_memory_estimator` (the hybrid layer count, the projector and the working set). | The estimate was the app's only word about whether a model fits, and it was wrong by 1.7 GB on exactly the model family that needs the answer: the user read "it does not fit" and lowered `--n-gpu-layers`, which put a third of the model on the CPU — the slowness they were trying to avoid. The truth is that the card was never the bottleneck (the ubatch was) and that the latency is the card's arithmetic, not its memory. The estimate now measures what the server reserves, the Launch tab says so out loud, and the shipped profile leaves a gigabyte of headroom on a 6 GB card without touching the vision budget or the long-table case. |
| 180  | **The fix protocol speaks span edits behind a deterministic diff gate (`span_hybrid`).** The fix task's reply contract changes from «FIX followed by the complete corrected block» (ADR 163) to **span_hybrid** (`docs/todo/fix_models_improvements.md` is the study, `ocr_diff_gate.py` the reference port): text-like blocks answer span edits — one `old_substring => new_substring` line per change — and the gate (`src/models/FixDiffGate`) applies them to the candidate itself, so unchanged text never passes through the model; `table`/`equation` keep the full block but must declare every change under a mandatory `EDITS:` section, and a word-level diff demotes any undeclared edit to Review. A FIX whose edits are all equivalence no-ops reads as Ok (the candidate is kept); an anchor that is missing or matches more than one place demotes to Review; the fourth verdict token **EMPTY** («the crop holds no readable text») lands on Review like the decision task's `empty_crop`. The gate's equivalence normalization (NFC, soft hyphen, ligatures, spaces, quotes — never е/ё or homoglyphs) mirrors the block prompts' «Equivalent (never fix)» lists, which stay synced with the decision v6 rules; the system prompt carries only the protocol — the four tokens, the narrowed REVIEW (ambiguity matters only where it can change the verdict) and the no-truncation rule («the reply would exceed the budget → REVIEW»). The byte-for-byte `formula`/`reference` prompt duplicates are gone (`blockAliases` serves them), and `key_value` — the decision vocabulary the fix table used to miss — ships its own prompt. `maxOutputByBlock` budgets the reply per block type (64 for a page number, 8192 for a table) and overrides the request profile's `max_tokens` only where the profile ships an entry; the `stop` request parameter ends the reply at the verdict. Tests: `test_fix_diff_gate` (the gate), `test_fix_protocol` (the wire and the parser), `test_model_preset_catalog::fixRolesShipTheCorrectionProtocol` (the profiles). Measured on 20.10.2026 with the full OCR_TEST corpus (194 real qwen3.5-4b fix replies, greedy decoding): the first strict gate drowned 35% of the blocks in Review — the model normalizes what it copies (it drops the `\\(…\\)` carrier delimiters and collapses the doubled spaces of the candidate), answers `FIX none` when it found nothing, writes `EDITS: none`, and prefixes cells `1c5:`/`2:` instead of `r1c5:`. The gate therefore matches anchors in an equivalence view with an origin map (carrier markup dropped, spacing collapsed — a match maps back to the real span), reads a no-changes FIX as the confirmation it meant, lets `EDITS: none` defer to the equivalence check, accepts the wild cell-location spellings, compares math chunks (with a backslash) space-insensitively, and never requires markup tokens to be declared. Same replies, gate only: Review 67 → 45, Fixed 24 → 35; the residual Reviews are the model's own protocol violations (template echoes, unverbatim anchors on misspelled words, undeclared real edits), addressed by the hardened prompts (copy character-for-character, minimal edits, OK when nothing needs changing). The gate cannot validate the *content* of `new`. A/B on the same 92-block sample, both through the final gate: the original prompts produced 18 fixes scoring +6 improved / −12 damaged against the ground truth (the fixer damaged twice as much as it repaired — «исправляет неправильно»), the hardened ones produce 10 fixes at +5/−5 — the minuscule-edit and verbatim-anchor wording made the fixer conservative, and its undisciplined replies land on Review (24, human eyes) instead of corrupting text. Equation blocks were the weak class (~45% Review, 88-second runaway generations): the OCR models emit spaced LaTeX ("Bi _ {1}", "\\frac {...") that provokes the 4B into listing hundreds of pure spacing rewrites until the reply truncates at the budget. Two counter-moves, both measured on the 50-formula sample: the wire candidate is normalized to canonical LaTeX spacing first (`FixDiffGate::normalizeLatexCandidate`, rendering-preserving — the churn trigger is gone, the mean generation fell from thousands of tokens to 85), and formulas answer the span contract from now on (only tables keep the full-block + EDITS: form — the model preferred span for formulas on its own even under the full-block prompt). In the math views (no-op checks, anchor matching, block equivalence) whitespace carries no content at all and `\\tag{n}` equals `(n)`, so whatever churn still arrives reads as Ok and keeps the candidate's bytes. Result: 33 Ok / 4 Fixed (+2/−2 vs the ground truth) / 13 Review (26%) — and no runaway generations; the residual Reviews are the model re-styling anchors it cannot copy, which lands on the human instead of the text. | A full-block FIX makes the model re-generate every already-correct character, and sampling drift corrupts precisely the text that was right — the measured failure mode behind the rework. Span edits make the correct text physically unable to pass through the model, cut the reply 10–50×, and turn a silent rewrite into an auditable, gated event; the structural blocks that cannot be expressed as spans carry the declared-edits audit instead. |
| 182  | **The request texts of a task live in their own profiles; the model profiles keep the parameters.** The fix and decision tasks send the same requests whatever model serves them — the four fix models carried byte-identical prompt sets, and the three decision models differed only in the calibration (thresholds) and the routing, not in the wording. The prompt texts therefore move out of the model profiles into **request profiles** under `resources/profiles/requests/` — `fix-requests.json` and `decision-requests.json` — and a model role names the one it speaks (`"promptProfile": "fix-requests"`). The model profiles keep everything that is genuinely per-model: the **launch** parameters, the **request** parameters (temperature, `max_tokens`, `stop`, …), the **output budget** (`maxOutput`, `maxOutputByBlock`), the **decision calibration** (`matchThreshold`, `matchThresholdByBlock`, `emptyThreshold`, `readableThreshold`) and the **routing rules**. `ModelProfiles` loads the request profiles from `:/profiles/requests` and merges their texts into the role, so every existing lookup (`systemPromptFor`, `blockPromptFor`, `decisionBlockFor`, `statePromptFor`, `questionFor`, `criteriaTrueFor`) keeps working unchanged; a role that names an unknown request profile is a load error, not a silent empty prompt. The OCR models (teleocr, glm-ocr, mineru, paddleocr-vl, lfm25-vl-3b, lightonocr) keep their own prompts inline — their tasks *do* differ per model. Tests: `test_model_preset_catalog::requestProfilesCarryTheSharedRequests` (the split: two request profiles, every fix/decision role names one and inherits its texts, the OCR roles keep theirs inline). | Four copies of the same fix system prompt is four places to edit and three chances to forget one — the kind of drift that only shows up as «the gemma model answers differently from the qwen one» months later. The split makes the shared wording a single file and leaves the model profile with the only data that is actually per-model, so a reader of `qwen3.5-9b.json` sees «this model is served by the fix request profile» instead of a wall of prompt text. The decision calibration stays with the models on purpose: the thresholds are measured per model (clef-flash 0.8, d3 0.85) and merging them into a shared file would have hidden a real difference. |
| 181  | **The fix protocol speaks three verdicts (OK / FIX / REVIEW), names the document language, and the formula block answers with the complete formula.** The `EMPTY` verdict is gone from the scheme and from the code (`FixModel::parseResponse` no longer has a branch for it, the profile system prompts no longer list it): a reply that is none of the three — an old `EMPTY`, a free-text remark, a protocol echo — is a **deviation** and lands on **Review** (the human's eyes) with the reason in the footer, instead of the failed status the ADR 180 parser gave a free-text reply. The **`FIX none`** reaction is gone too: the model declaring «nothing to change» while choosing a verdict is a deviation, not a confirmation, so it lands on Review rather than on Ok (the ADR 180 gate read it as the confirmation it meant — the measured qwen3.5-4b habit, but a block the model never judged must not be marked verified). The system prompt of the four fix profiles is rewritten around **six independent axes** (script, case, face, position, delimiters, designations) instead of the three-level completeness/characters/layout comparison, and it states the rule that decides the verdict: fix an axis only when the image shows a difference, leave it alone otherwise. It names the **document language** through a `{{LANGUAGE}}` placeholder, filled in by the app with the document's language (Russian for now; the choice and the auto-detection come in the next step). The **formula** block prompt changes with it: the formula answers with the **complete formula as LaTeX** (not span edits) and the gate applies that block as the correction — `FixDiffGate::fixFullBlock` reads a math reply without an `EDITS:` section as the corrected formula, after the equivalence check has ruled out a pure respacing (the math view treats the display wrapper as carrier, so `$$x$$`, `\[x\]` and `x` are the same formula). Tests: `test_fix_protocol` (the parser: `offSchemeReplyIsReviewed`, `garbageIsReviewed`, `fixNoneIsReviewed`, `formulaReplyIsAppliedAsTheCorrection`), `test_fix_diff_gate` (the gate: `fixNoneIsReviewed`, `spanFullBlockFallbackForFormulas`), `test_model_preset_catalog::fixRolesShipTheCorrectionProtocol` (the profiles: the three tokens, the placeholder, the formula prompt). | The four-token scheme was a workaround for a contract the model kept breaking: `EMPTY` existed so that «the crop is empty» had a home, and the parser had to guess which deviation was which. Folding the empty crop into REVIEW (the new prompt says so: «Use REVIEW if any relevant part is unreadable, cropped, or ambiguous, or image is empty») and letting every other deviation land on Review gives one rule — a verdict the app can trust or a block the human looks at — instead of three special cases. Dropping the `FIX none» confirmation matters most: that reaction marked a block verified on the strength of a model that never looked at the image, which is the one thing the decision pass exists to prevent. The six axes replace the completeness/characters/layout triple because a technical block is wrong in one dimension at a time (a Cyrillic subscript, an italic that should be upright, a `\left` without its `\right`), and the new prompt says so per axis instead of asking the model to compare everything at once. |
| 183  | **The decision task asks ONE question and checks the block along independent axes; the formula block is the reference implementation.** The decision request profile (`resources/profiles/requests/decision-requests.json`) drops the v6 two-question dict (`match` noul + `diagnosis` choice) and ships a single noul `match` again: measured on 18.10.2026, **d3 answers every question of a request with its own forward pass** (~1.4 s each), so the second question doubled the price of a check — and the check is the hot path of the whole pipeline. The `diagnosis` question existed to route an empty or unreadable crop to **Review**; that route is not lost but moved: the fix task's own system prompt already answers **REVIEW** for a crop it cannot read, so a block whose text cannot be judged lands on human eyes there instead of on a fixer call. The **axes** come from the fix task (ADR 181): the state text (`state.notation`) names the independent axes — script, case, face, position, delimiters, designations, units — and says that a block is verified only if it is right on **all** of them, so one failed axis makes the block defective. The per-block rules were reduced to what is specific to the block type and now **add** to the general ones instead of replacing them (`VerificationQueueController::decisionNotation()` joins them), which is what makes the wording work on any language: the old v6 prompts hardcoded the Russian homoglyph lists («Russian е/ё, и/й», «Cyrillic vs Latin (а/a, в/B, …)») into every block type, and the new text states the rule once — «letters must belong to the alphabet of the document language» — with the concrete pairs left to the language-specific layer. The **document language** rides along in the request through a `{{LANGUAGE}}` placeholder (Russian for now, the same constant the fix prompt uses). The **formula** block is the reference implementation of the scheme: it is the block type the axis list was written for (the OCR models' spaced LaTeX «Bi _ {1}», the Greek-vs-Latin look-alikes, the integral signs, the tag) and the one the next measurements target first. The decision request carries the **canonical wire form** of a formula — the same `FixDiffGate::normalizeLatexCandidate` the fix task gets (ADR 180), so both tasks judge the same text and the checker does not spend its attention on the OCR models' LaTeX spacing; the block's own bytes stay untouched. Tests: `test_decision_model::requestBodyCarriesExactlyOneQuestion` (the wire carries one question), `test_app_import::decisionRequestCarriesTheNormalizedFormula` (the formula arrives in its canonical wire form, the block's text keeps its bytes), `test_model_preset_catalog::decisionRoleShipsTheStructuredStateWording` (the profiles: one question, the axes in the state text, the language placeholder). | The v6 split bought two things at the price of doubling the decision latency: a second opinion on the verdict and the empty/unreadable route to Review. The second opinion was the *choice* head vetoing a confident noul, and the axes already carry that veto in one pass — a model that answers «yes, the block matches» after checking the script, the case, the face, the position, the delimiters, the designations and the units has said more than a bare noul plus a class label. The empty/unreadable route was the price of the second question, and the fix task's REVIEW verdict pays it without one. The language was the missing parameter of the old prompts: they assumed Russian letters, so the same text could not judge an English or German document — naming the language once and stating the rule («a letter of another alphabet that looks identical is still a different letter») makes the profile reusable, and the per-block lists shrink to what is really per block. |
| 184  | **The decision request texts are rewritten around «the markup is a carrier», and every decision model's thresholds are re-measured on the corpus that matches the shipped request.** (a) The shared request profile `resources/profiles/requests/decision-requests.json` says, per block type, what the carrier markup is (`\(…\)`, `\[…\]`, LaTeX commands, HTML tags, Markdown — never content), lists the differences that **are** content (the letters of a word, a digit read as a letter, the decimal comma against the point, a missing word or punctuation mark), and asks the model to answer «no» only when it can point to a difference it sees in the crop. The formula reaches the checker in its canonical wire form (ADR 183: the same `FixDiffGate::normalizeLatexCandidate` the fix task gets), so the OCR models' spaced LaTeX no longer reads as noise and both tasks judge the same text. (b) The per-model calibration (`matchThresholdByBlock`) is **re-measured, not carried over**: the texts are shared by all three decision models, so a rewrite of the request moves every model's p(match) distribution. d3-mini on the OCRtest corpus (iteration 6, the run made with the shipped request): text 0.85, equation/formula 0.70, table 0.65, table_caption 0.80, table_footnote 0.45, list 0.75, the rest 0.85 → **FN 12 / FP 22** — the same false-alarm cost as the previous flat 0.85 (FN 14 / FP 22) with two fewer missed errors. clef-flash and d3-flash were re-measured the same way (iteration 7): clef text 0.80, equation/formula 0.75, table 0.50, table_caption 0.80, table_footnote 0.75, title 0.85 → **FN 22 / FP 25**; d3-flash text 0.80, equation/formula 0.75, table 0.90, table_caption 0.90, table_footnote 0.80, title 0.55 → **FN 34 / FP 7**. The thresholds are not hand-picked: `scripts/decision-eval/pick-thresholds.py` takes the knee of each type's curve — the last step where one caught error costs at most one false alarm — and the rule reproduces d3-mini's shipped profile exactly on its own run. (c) The measurement does not need the GUI: `scripts/decision-eval/score-project.py` sends the app's own request (the `v0_app_current` variant) over the corpus's crops and writes the verdicts back into a `.llocr` project, and it reproduces the app's numbers **bit for bit** — the state is a `QJsonObject`, so its keys are sorted, and the server serializes the state into the prompt as it arrives: an unsorted state is a different prompt and a different answer. The stand's port of the LaTeX normalization is pinned to the app's own output (`check_normalization_matches_the_app`), and the run aborts if the two drift apart. (d) The calibration curve comes from the stored P(match) (ADR 173), so a threshold sweep needs no new run: `scripts/decision-calibrate.py` prints FN/FP(t) per bucket and per block type, and `--policy` scores a profile's thresholds on a run. The shared wording has a price: the rewritten prompts were written for d3-mini, and on running text clef now answers worse than chance (AUC 0.479 against 0.860 of iteration 4) — thresholds cannot undo that, only a wording of its own for clef can, and the wording is shared by decision (ADR 182). Tests: `test_app_import::decisionRequestCarriesTheNormalizedFormula` (the canonical formula on the wire, the block's bytes untouched). | The normalization alone was not enough: with the old prompts it put **every clean formula into the flags** (FN 0 / FP 29 at 0.85) — the model read the LaTeX spacing as a difference — and the thresholds taken from that run were wrong for the request that shipped. A threshold is a measurement of one model's answer distribution under one request; change the request and the measurement is void, not slightly off. The old thresholds would have shipped as «calibrated» while describing a request that no longer exists. And the measurement had to be reproducible outside the GUI: the state key order showed that the app's request is not just «the same fields» but a specific byte sequence, and only a stand that reproduces it exactly can re-measure a model without a human clicking through the app. |
| 185  | **The block panel tells the two layers apart: the fix outcome, the score, the layer in view and the user's own verdict.** (a) The panel's title is the block label alone — the «Block» prefix is gone. (b) **The fix outcome is stored, not inferred**: `BoxFixStatus` (`None` / `NotNeeded` / `Fixed` / `Damaged`) rides on the block, because the correction layer alone cannot tell a confirmed text from a stored correction — the old code stored a duplicate of the text and could not say afterwards whether the fixer had confirmed the block or had never seen it. A fix that answers **OK** now stores **nothing** and does **not** touch the text layer's verdict: the status stays as after the recognition (ADR 164's two layers become visible instead of one of them being erased), and the panel shows the outcome as «Не требует исправления». A **FIX** stores the correction and shows «Исправлено», a **REVIEW** marks the block damaged and shows «Повреждено». A confirmed text is therefore never shown as a «corrected text» — there is no correction to show. (c) **The panel carries the check score and the overall status**: the score of the layer in view (the P(match) the decision model produced, ADR 173), the fix outcome and one overall word — «Распознан» (nothing checked it), «Проверен» (the layer in view carries a verdict — also after a check that followed a fix), «Исправлен» (a correction the decision has not judged yet) or «Утверждён» (the user confirmed it by hand). Each section carries the verdict of **its own** layer, so a corrected block shows the rejected OCR text and the correction with two independent statuses. (d) **The layer toggle**: `BoundingBox::useCorrectedText` picks the layer the user reads; the page text (the preview, the Markdown, the export) follows it, the verdict and the overlay dot follow it, and a re-fix that produces a new text takes the view (the user was reading the old one). The toggle is a per-block choice, persisted in `project.json` next to the fix outcome — both fields are **optional** (a project written before them reads the defaults: no fix ran, the correction is in view), so the schema version stays at 2. **«Отменить исправление» follows the same rule**: the reverted correction must leave the page text too — it did not (the revert path kept an un-migrated copy of the old baseline handling and never wrote the text back), so the preview kept the correction the block had just dropped. (e) **The user's verdict**: `BoxCheckStatus::Approved` (value 5) marks the layer in view as correct even when the decision model rejected it — the escape hatch for a decision that got the block or the correction wrong. An approved block is not a problem block anymore, and it is neither verified nor refuted by the model: its score stays the model's, but the panel does not show a score for it. Tests: `test_box_transitions` (the fix outcomes, the confirmation keeping the recognition's verdict, the toggle, the approval), `test_box_model` (the new roles), `test_project_store` (the round-trip), `test_app_import` (the fix outcomes through the real controller, the approval, the toggle moving the preview text). | The panel answered one question with one word, and the two layers of a block (ADR 164) fought over it: the correction won as soon as it existed, so a block the decision had rejected looked «Исправлено» while its verdict was a gray «nothing», and a fix that confirmed the text threw the verdict away — the user could not tell a confirmed block from a never-checked one. The fix outcome is a fact about the block, not a derivation from its layers, so it is stored; the verdict of a layer is a fact about that layer, so both are shown and neither erases the other. The toggle exists because the two layers are two **readings** of the same block: the user must be able to compare them, and the document must carry the one they chose — otherwise the preview would silently disagree with the panel. The approval exists because the decision model is a model: when it rejects a correct block, the human needs a way to say so, and that verdict must be distinguishable from the model's own «Ok» — otherwise a misjudged block would keep its red mark and the badge would keep counting it as a problem. |
| 186  | **The block panel becomes a version inspector: two cards, one status each, and the changes tinted in place.** (a) **Two version cards instead of a flat text and a toggle button.** The panel shows the recognized text (OCR) and the corrected text (AI) as two cards; the card in view is the one the document carries — it wears the accent (border, stripe, radio) and says «Используется в документе», the other one is muted and says «Нажмите, чтобы использовать этот вариант». Clicking a card switches the layer (`useCorrectedText`), and the preview, the export and the overlay dot follow it (ADR 185). The «Показать оригинал» / «Показать исправление» buttons are gone: the card *is* the switch. (b) **Every status has exactly one place.** The header carries the state of the block — one word: «Распознан», «Проверен», «Исправлен», «Утверждён», «Не совпал», «Повреждён» — and the card headers carry the verdict and the score of *their own* layer (the word and the P(match), ADR 173). The separate «Исправление: …» row is gone: a fix that stored nothing shows «Не требует исправления» next to the state, a fix that stored a correction shows the correction itself. (c) **The changes are tinted in place.** The core compares the two versions word by word (`core/TextDiff.h`): the fragments the correction dropped are tinted in the original (soft red), the ones it put in are tinted in the correction (soft green), and never with a strikethrough — a strike reads as a minus sign inside a formula. A word is a run of letters, digits and the characters that belong to a word inside LaTeX (`\`, `_`, `.`), so a rewritten formula differs in one fragment and not in the whole line. The «Различия» switch turns the tint off for reading the plain code; a text too long to compare is shown without a tint. (d) **The code-like blocks are read in a monospace face** (`Theme.mono`): a formula, a table, a code block or a snippet — the brackets, the subscripts and the spaces must not drift apart. (e) **The operator’s actions.** «Утвердить блок» / «Утвердить исправление» is the primary (green) action and approves the version in view; «Сбросить исправление» is the secondary one beside it. `Ctrl+Return` / `Cmd+Return` approves the version in view and jumps to the next problem block of the page — the shortcut stands down while the page text editor holds the keyboard, because there the Return belongs to the text. (f) **The user may correct a version by hand.** The card’s «Изм.» turns the text into an editable field: what the user writes is stored in that version, the version is marked «Утверждён» (a hand-written text needs no verdict) and becomes the version in view; the page text follows the blocks, exactly as after a correction. | The panel showed the same block four times in four words, and the two texts looked alike: the operator could not see what the correction had changed without reading two LaTeX walls side by side, and could not tell which of them the document carried. A version is a *choice*, so it is drawn as a choice: a card the user can click, with the one in view marked as such. A status is a *fact about one layer*, so it is written once, on the layer it belongs to. The difference between the two versions is the very thing the panel exists to show, so it is tinted where it happens — a diff that scales with the number of changes, unlike a list of them. The monospace face is the plain fact that a formula is code. The approval is a human verdict, so it stays a separate gesture from the check, and the hotkey exists because the operator works through blocks, not through clicks. |
| 187  | **The approval can be revoked, the hand-written text is its own state, and the card has a uniform frame.** (a) **«Отозвать утверждение».** The confirm button of the panel is one button with two states: while the block carries the user's confirmation it reads «Отозвать утверждение», and the click undoes it — the verdict the decision model gave stands again («Не совпал» is back, the block counts as a problem block again). To make the undo possible the block keeps the verdict the approval replaced (`BoundingBox::checkStatusBeforeApproval` / `correctedCheckStatusBeforeApproval`); a new verdict forgets it, so a stale verdict can never come back, and both fields travel in `project.json` as optional keys (the schema version stays 2 — a project written before them reads the default). (b) **A hand-written text is not a confirmation.** The status of a manually edited version is a new value — `BoxCheckStatus::Edited` («Изменён вручную»), not `Approved`: the text is the user's own, not a confirmation that the model's verdict was wrong, and the two facts must not be read as one. A human state carries no score: the P(match) belongs to the model's verdict, so the badge of a confirmed or a hand-written version shows the word alone. (c) **The edit has an explicit exit.** The «Изм.» button becomes «Сохранить» while the version is being edited — the way out of the edit is on the button, not in a click outside the text; `Esc` cancels the edit, and the card's `Ctrl`/`Cmd+Return` saves instead of the panel's shortcut approving the block (a shortcut must not approve a block while a text is being typed in). (d) **The card has a uniform frame.** The colored stripe on the left is gone: the version in view is marked by the accent border and the radio dot, and the frame is one thickness all around the perimeter. | A confirmation the operator cannot take back is a dead end: the model rejected the block, the user accepted it, and the block's red mark stayed — the only way out was to re-check the block, which costs a model call and can answer differently. The undo has to restore the *model's* verdict, not an empty state, so the block keeps it. A hand-written text was marked «Утверждён», which claims the model was wrong — but the operator did not check anything, they replaced the text: the state is their own and deserves its own word. The exit from the edit was a click outside the text — invisible and easy to miss: the button that starts the edit must say what it does while the edit is on. The stripe on the left made the frame two-thick on one side; the accent border and the radio dot already say which version is in view. |
| 188  | **The panel loses its colour noise, the «Различия» switch waits for a second version, and the «Исправить» badge counts the fixer's work.** (a) **No green in the panel** (ADR 186 amended): the confirm button and the «Сохранить» button are ordinary buttons — the same surface and frame the unchecked «Различия» switch has. The green «primary» variant of `LLOButton` is gone: the panel's colours are the verdicts (green / red / grey) and the accent of the version in view, nothing else. (b) **«Различия» appears only when there is a second version**: the switch compares the two versions, so without a correction there is nothing to compare — the button is hidden, and the corrected card is the only thing that stands next to the OCR one. (c) **The «Исправить (N)» badge counts the blocks that still wait for the fixer** (ADR 167 amended): a block leaves the count when the fixer has answered its candidate — the fix confirmed the text (OK) or could not read it (REVIEW); such a block waits for the operator, not for another fix run. A stored correction is a new candidate: a correction the decision model rejects goes back to the fixer and into the count again. The count and the fix queue are one set (`BoxTransitions::needsFix`, `PageStatus::pendingFixCount`, `VerificationQueueController::collectFixBoxes`), so the number is the work the button does. The re-recognition («Распознать проблемные блоки») is a different remedy and keeps the old set: the decision model still rejects those blocks, so the OCR model may read them again. The availability property `scopedProblemBlockCount` is renamed `scopedPendingFixCount` (ADR 167's row keeps the old name); the page status — the red mark on the thumbnail — is untouched: a rejected block still needs the operator even after the fixer answered it. | The panel is read for hours: a green button that says nothing but «the action of this moment» pulls the eye off the verdicts, which are the information the panel carries. The «Различия» switch promises a comparison; with a single version it is a switch to nowhere, and the operator has to guess why it does nothing. The «Исправить» badge counted the decision model's rejections, so it kept its number and its warm highlight after the fixer had already answered every one of them — and the next click spent another round of model calls to get the same answers. The badge must count the work that is left, not the blocks that were once wrong. |
| 189  | **A click on a block selects it; the block moves only in the edit mode; a new block is drawn, not dropped.** (a) **A click selects.** The block's mouse area no longer moves the block: it selects it (the block panel opens) and a drag that starts on a block moves the page, exactly as a drag on the empty page does — the pan lives in the preview's own functions (`beginPan` / `continuePan`), and the page's mouse area and the blocks' one share it. The eight resize handles appear only in the edit mode. (b) **The edit mode is a button of the block's mini toolbar.** Its first button opens the block for editing: the icon is «move», the block is dragged and its handles resize it. In the mode the button becomes a checkmark; pressing it again leaves the mode, and so does a click outside the block (the empty page deselects the block, another block takes the selection). The mode belongs to the selected block: another selection closes it, and so does a page change. (c) **A new block is drawn on the page.** The toolbar's add buttons and the block-type menu arm the draw mode instead of dropping a block at a default place: the cursor turns into a crosshair, a chip follows it and names the block being drawn, and the drag paints the rectangle in the hue the block will wear. The release creates the block with that rectangle (`AppController::addBlockAt`, clamped into the page) and selects it; a click without a drag draws nothing, and `Esc` or a second click on the same button cancels the mode. (d) **A block without content says so.** The panel's status word for a block that carries no text and no verdict is «Разметка» (markup) — the word the page uses for itself («Только разметка») — and the dot disappears, as it does on the overlay: `BoxCheckStatus::NotChecked` means «with text — recognized, without text — layout markup (no dot)». A block the user drew and nothing has been recognized in yet is not a recognized text. | The blocks were moved by accident: the user panned the page with a press on a block, and the block went with the pointer — the geometry is a fact the crop, the recognition and the export depend on, and a stray drag changes it silently. Splitting the two gestures gives each of them a name and a place: a click selects, the toolbar's button says «move» while the block waits for the pointer, the checkmark says the work is done. The draw mode follows the same rule — a block dropped at a default place lands where nobody meant it to be, while a drawn rectangle says where it belongs. |
| 190  | **A structural change is not a manual edit: the page text keeps following the blocks.** A removed block no longer marks the page as manually edited — the marker means exactly one thing now: *the user typed the text by hand*. It is set by `setCurrentPageText()` alone and cleared by Revert; a removal (like a block landing) only moves the revert target forward, so the removed block's text never comes back. The page text is written again by the next recognition, fix or re-check. Two rules keep the user's own text safe: a removal never replaces it (the blocks change, the text under the panel is the user's), and a pipeline change that lands on a hand-edited page is **merged** into that text — the common part anchors the change, the user's wording wins where it diverges — instead of being dropped. (ADR 177 amended: the added block no longer lands at a default place either — ADR 189 draws it.) (ADR 177 amended: the added block no longer lands at a default place either — ADR 189 draws it.) | The marker did two jobs at once: «the text is not the recognition» and «the text is the user's own». Only the second may freeze the page text. A removal froze it: the operator drew a block, recognized it, and the text never reached the panel — the block was in the preview and in the model, but the MD view kept the old text, so the work looked lost. The freeze was then traded for a silent drop: on a hand-edited page the pipeline's content was thrown away. The merge ends the trade: the user's text and the pipeline's content both survive. The same rule already governed a block landing (ADR 07f34da: «recognition and fix produce the page text themselves — this must not read as a manual edit»); a removal had simply been left behind. Tests: `TestAppImport::drawnBlockReachesThePageTextAfterARemoval` (the operator's flow), plus the removal tests re-pointed at the new contract. |
| 191  | **The quant dropdown answers the question the user actually has: what card does this quantization need.** The item reads `✓ Q8_0 — 9.1 GiB · VRAM ≥ 11.4 GiB`: the marker (✓ / ⬇) carries the status, the file size stays, and the **minimum video memory** of the whole server follows. The parenthetical is gone — the status word repeated the marker and the family hint («макс. качество») stood where a number belongs. **The requirement is known before the file is downloaded**: the model profile ships the architecture (a new `memory` block: the layers, the KV shape, the embedding width, the feed-forward width, the mixture-of-experts terms, the image budget) and the curated sizes (the quantization's `sizeBytes`, the projector's `sizeBytes`, the draft's `draftBytes`), so every quantization of every model carries its own number. **One formula for both paths**: `ModelMemoryEstimator` works on a *shape* (the weights, the projector, the draft, the architecture) and a *budget* (the context, the ubatch, the cache types, the image tokens) and returns the buffers (the KV cache, the working set, the vision encoder) plus the server's overhead — the file path reads the shape from the GGUF header, the profile path reads it from the profile, and the two agree. The numbers are llama.cpp's own (b11799, `--parallel 1`, the flags the app launches the server with): the KV-cache formula is **exact** for the plain models, the working set lands within 12 % (a 12 % margin turns it into a requirement), and the **vision encoder's buffer is measured per image token** from llama.cpp's own clip reservation (the GLM-OCR tower costs 233 KiB per token, the Qwen3-VL one 276 KiB, the deepseek-ocr one 303 KiB, the LFM2.5-VL one 121 KiB). The **image budget** follows what the task sends: a page fills the model's own budget (the model shrinks the page to its `image-max-tokens`), a block crop is scaled up to the floor the model declares (its `image-min-tokens`). Two projectors do not follow the token count, and both carry the measured cost of the whole encoder instead of a rate: the **gemma-4-E4B** runs without flash attention, so its encoder holds an attention matrix over every patch of the page (3.7 GiB at the app's 1120-token budget — thirteen times its warmup reservation), and the **Unlimited-OCR** projector tiles the page into three windows plus a global view (2.8 GiB). **A draft that is a model of its own runs in its own context**: the LFM2.5-VL's DSpark drafter and the gemma-4 MTP drafters are separate GGUF files, and llama.cpp gives each its own KV cache and its own compute buffer (the DSpark drafter: 541 MiB of weights, a 128 MiB cache and a 508 MiB compute buffer). The profile carries the draft's weights *and* the cost of its context (`draftKvBytesPerToken`, `draftContextBytes`); the MTP block inside a model (the Qwen3.5) has no context of its own. **A sliding-window hybrid keeps a cache that does not follow the context**: the gemma-4 models give their sliding layers a fixed number of cells, so the profile carries that part apart (`kvBytesFixed`) and only the full-attention layers scale. The cases llama.cpp reserves differently are **measured, not modelled**: a sliding-window hybrid (gemma-4) keeps a cache it allocates by cells, a mixture of experts (Unlimited-OCR) materializes the six experts it routes a token to. The Launch tab shows the same breakdown with the new terms (the draft, the vision encoder, the overhead) and compares the **requirement**, not the bare buffers, with the card. A model outside the catalog names no card — nothing is known about the encoder it would run. Three fixes fell out of the measurement: the speculative-decoding head (the `nextn` block) keeps **no** KV cache — llama.cpp runs it as a separate graph — and the KV-layer count no longer counts it; the draft's weights were declared in the profile but never read, so the draft cost nothing; and the gemma-4-12B's key width was four times the real one (the key length is the total across the heads), which overstated its cache. Tests: `test_model_memory_estimator` (the vision encoder, the overhead, the draft's context, the sliding-window cache, the MTP block, the profiles, the image budget, the launch profile), `test_model_installer` (the dropdown carries the requirement). The method, the calibration of every shipped model and the checklist for a new one are in `docs/27-memory-estimate.md`, with the measurement scripts `scripts/measure-memory.sh` and `scripts/memory-from-log.py`. | The dropdown said how big the file is, but the file is not what the card has to hold: the projector, the KV cache, the working set and the vision encoder add up to more than the weights, and for a page model the vision encoder alone can be larger than the model (GLM-OCR's encoder reserves 1.4 GiB for a 6144-token page). The user chose a quantization by a number that did not answer the question, and the estimate behind it was right for one model family only — it read the architecture from a file that a quantization that is not downloaded yet does not have, and it ignored the vision encoder, the draft and the overhead. The requirement is a *fact about the whole server*, so it is computed from the whole server: the profile carries what the file would have said, llama.cpp's own report says how much the server reserves, and the two paths (the installed file, the profile) end up with the same number. The parenthetical went because a dropdown row is not a sentence: the marker already says whether the file is there, and the family hint was the one thing in the row that said nothing about the machine. |
| 192  | **The fix protocol speaks one language again: after FIX comes the complete corrected block.** The `span_hybrid` contract of ADR 180 is withdrawn — the span edits (`old => new`), the declared-`EDITS:` audit for tables and the anchor machinery around them are gone; a FIX reply carries **the complete corrected block** and nothing else, for every block type. The gate (`src/models/FixDiffGate`) shrinks to the one question the reply leaves open: may that block replace the candidate? An **equivalent** block keeps the candidate's own bytes (the model re-spelled it, changed nothing) and reads as Ok; an **empty** reply, a `FIX none` and a reply **cut by the token budget** (`finish_reason: length`) land on Review — a fragment is not a correction; everything else is the correction. The equivalence view mirrors the block prompts' «Never fix» lists (NFC, soft hyphen, ligatures, ellipsis, spaces, quotes, a re-wrapped line, a hyphen at a line end) and never touches the strictly different pairs (е/ё, homoglyphs, the decimal comma), so a cosmetic rewrite cannot pass as a fix. The prompt texts move to the **refined v2 profile** (`resources/profiles/requests/fix-requests.json`): the six axes (script, case, face, position, delimiters, unit integrity), the per-type «Mode after FIX: the complete corrected block», the merged caption and footnote types (the aliases `image_caption`/`table_caption` → `caption` and `figure_footnote`/`table_footnote` → `footnote` follow), and the formula and table rules that keep the macro style, the operand order and the cell positions. The reply budget follows the payload: `maxOutputByBlock` now has to fit the whole block (a text block 1024 → 2048 tokens, a list 2048, code 4096, a table 8192), because the whole block travels in the reply — and the budgets live in the **request profile** (`fix-requests.json`), one copy for all four fix models, since the budget is the protocol's rule and not a model's property; a model profile that ships its own entry for a block type keeps it (the shared values fill the gaps). The decision task is untouched — its thresholds are measured under its own request (ADR 184), so rewriting that request would void the measurement. Tests: `test_fix_diff_gate` (the gate), `test_fix_protocol` (the wire, the parser, the budget cut), `test_model_preset_catalog::fixRolesShipTheCorrectionProtocol` (the profiles), `test_app_import` (the fix run end to end). The stand's port of the gate (`OCRtest/fix_models/gate.py`) follows the app, so the measurement still counts what the application applies. | The span contract was a good idea for a good model and a bad one for the models that actually serve this task: the 4B models could not carry the anchor («old_substring => new_substring») — they dropped the anchor, invented the cell locations (`1c5:` instead of `r1c5:`), or answered a span edit for a whole block, so most of the traffic landed on Review and the measured fixes damaged as much text as they repaired. The full-block reply is the simpler contract: one payload, no anchors, no declarations, and a gate that only has to know whether the block the model wrote may replace the block the OCR wrote. The price is honest — the model now re-writes the whole block, so the protection against churn moves from the diff machinery to the equivalence view (the candidate's bytes survive a cosmetic rewrite) and to the budget (a cut reply is a fragment and lands on the human, not on the block). |
| 193  | **The fix task may think first: an optional reasoning mode with four steps.** The **model profile** says whether the model can think at all (`reasoning: true` on the fix role — the qwen3.5 pair and the gemma pair both do: the gemma chat template carries the `enable_thinking` switch and the thought channel, and llama.cpp b11799 splits the thought into `reasoning_content` and honours the per-request budget — the option had simply never been added to the gemma profiles), the **request profile** holds the steps the task shares (`reasoningModes`: off / low 512 / med 1024 / high 1536), and the app derives the request parameter `reasoning_budget_tokens` from the two. The budget rides the request: llama.cpp reads `reasoning_budget_tokens` per request and it overrides the server's `--reasoning-budget`, so **no server restart is needed** — the mode takes effect on the next fix. The budget is also the switch: `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking` follows it, and zero keeps the old behaviour (the reply is the answer). **The generation budget is computed inside the server's context window** (`src/core/RequestBudget.h`): the answer's block budget plus the thinking, clipped so the request fits — and the thinking yields before the answer, because a correction matters more than a thought. The window comes from the running server's launch configuration (`ctx-size`), the prompt's size is estimated (the text at two characters per token for Cyrillic and four for the rest, the image at the model's token floor). A cut answer is a Review, not a correction, so the request has to fit: when the thinking does not fit, it shrinks and the footer says by how much. The mode is a request parameter like temperature or max_tokens: it is stored per model in the user's request profile and edited in Settings → Models → ⚙ → Request, where a parameter with named options shows a dropdown. The measurement behind it: the stand (`OCRtest/fix_models`) — thinking 512 tokens lifted the fix index from 0.54 to 0.65 on the problem page, at the price of speed. | The span contract failed because the small models could not carry it; the same reasoning applies to the reasoning mode: the cheapest thing that works is a number in the request and a switch that follows it. The budget belongs in the request, not in the server's launch flags, because llama.cpp reads it per request — a mode that needs a restart is a mode the user forgets to apply. The capability stays in the model profile, because only the model knows whether it was trained to think: a mode that the template ignores would cost time and buy nothing. And the budget cannot be a plain sum: the thinking tokens come out of the same context as the answer, so a mode that only adds tokens silently eats the answer — the correction is the point of the task, the thought is not. |

| 194  | **A formula is normalized where it is recognized, and a formula whose LaTeX is broken is rejected on the spot.** The canonical form of a formula's text (`core/LatexText`: «Bi _ {1}» → «Bi _{1}», «\frac {...}» → «\frac{...}») is no longer a **wire** transformation — the block itself carries it from the moment the recognition lands (`BoxTransitions::latexResultApplied`), so the panel, the export, the checker and the fixer all see the same bytes, and the old `FixDiffGate::normalizeLatexCandidate` is gone. **The syntax of a formula is checked without a LaTeX engine** (`LatexText::check`): the braces, the environments, the `\left`/`\right` pairs, the math delimiters, a command cut off at the end, an argument missing from `\frac`/`\text`/`\sqrt`. A formula that fails is **rejected right away**: the status is **«неверный синтаксис»** (red, `BoxCheckStatus::InvalidSyntax`), the panel names what is broken (the kind, not the wording), and the block **is excluded from the verification and from the fix** — no model is asked about a text that cannot be rendered; it waits for a new recognition (the page's «Распознать заново» picks it up). The check is deliberately conservative: only what certainly cannot be rendered is a defect (a lone «$» that starts and ends nothing is a currency sign, not a delimiter). The formulas of the recognized page are checked the same way as a re-recognized block. The test of ADR 183/184 changed its name with it: `test_app_import::decisionRequestCarriesTheNormalizedFormula` is now `theFormulaLandsCanonicalAndTheWireCarriesIt`. |

| 195  | **A markdown code fence in a formula is carrier markup, not formula content.** The OCR models answer a formula as «$$```latex … ```$$» — sometimes in several pieces and sometimes inside the math wrapper — and a fence inside a formula cannot be rendered: the backtick is not a LaTeX character, so the formula breaks in the preview, in the export and in every model that reads it. The fence is **unwrapped when the formula lands** (`LatexText::canonical`, the same moment the canonical spacing is applied — ADR 194), and the LaTeX inside is kept: «$$\n```latex\nK_{\ell} = \frac{1}{R^*}\n```\n$$» becomes the bare formula. A fence that could not be unwrapped (an unbalanced one) and a leftover backtick are a defect of their own: the status **«неверный синтаксис»** with the reason **«блок кода внутри формулы»** (`LatexText::Problem::CodeMarkup`). **The page text follows the blocks**: the parser builds the page text from the boxes it has just created, so the text is built from them again after the canonical form was applied (`AppController`: `applyFormulaVerdicts` → `rebuildTextFromBlocks`) — the model's spacing and the fence must not survive into the document, the preview or the export. The reply parser already stripped an **outer** fence (`VisionChatModel::stripOuterCodeFence`) — the nested fence inside the math wrapper was not covered, and it is the nested one that breaks a formula. Tests: `test_latex_text::aMarkdownFenceIsCarrierNotFormula`, `test_app_import::aFormulaInAMarkdownFenceLandsWithoutTheFence`. |

| 196  | **The carriers of a formula are read as carriers, and the invisible delimiter pairs with any bracket.** Two defects found on the app's own formula corpus (the page «5_5_5_eq_insane»: 16 formulas) and fixed together. **(a) A formula the model split into display-math fragments.** One equation arrives as several «$$…$$» blocks — the «$$» between the lines of one equation. The renderer wrapped the whole text in one «$$…$$», so the math ended at the first inner «$$» and the rest of the formula stayed **plain text**: the equation did not render at all, and nothing said so. Now every fragment is a line of the formula and gets **its own display block**, and the equation number that stands on its own after the last line becomes the **\tag** of the formula (the app's convention) — the lines of (63а) now render as the book prints them. The check does not call such a formula broken: the «$$» are carriers, and they are balanced. **The verdict is derived from the text, so a project written before the rules changed is recomputed when it opens** (`BoxTransitions::syntaxVerdictRefreshed`): a formula that is sound again is no longer rejected, and one that is broken is rejected whatever the file says — the two false red blocks of that page are gone on the next open. **(b) The invisible delimiter.** «\left[ … \right. \\ \left. … \right]» is how a bracket is split across the lines of a formula; the invisible delimiter closes any opening delimiter and is closed by any of them. The check compared the delimiters literally and called two **valid** multi-line formulas broken — the two red warnings on that page were both false, while the three formulas that really did not render said nothing. Tests: `test_det_parser::rendersFragmentedEquationsAsDisplayBlocks`, `test_latex_text::theInvisibleDelimiterPairsWithAnyBracket`, `test_latex_text::aFragmentedFormulaIsNotASyntaxProblem`. | The carriers are the app's own job: a formula is recognized as a formula, and the text it carries is read with that in view. A formula that does not render is a hole in the document, and the app was quiet about it while flagging two healthy formulas — the worst of both. The page that found it is the app's own worst-case corpus («eq_insane»), so the corpus earns its keep: the checks now run against the shapes the models really produce, not against the shapes one imagines. |


| 197  | **The manifest of a project names files inside the project — nothing else.** The `file` field of a `.llocr` manifest is an untrusted input: it is no longer pasted into the extraction path, but validated (a relative path, no `.` or `..` segment, no drive letter, and the resolved path stays inside the extraction folder). A project that names a file outside itself is opened without that source, and the panel says so. The archive itself was already protected (ADR 34's extractor refuses `..`, absolute paths and symlink entries) — the manifest is a second, separate input and is now guarded on its own. The reason: `QDir::filePath()` neither normalizes `..` nor refuses an absolute argument, so `"file": "/Users/me/Documents/private.pdf"` used to make the app open and render a file outside the project. Test: `TestProjectStore::manifestFileOutsideTheProjectIsNotOpened`. |

| 198  | **A broken model index is rebuilt from the models on disk — never overwritten from an empty list.** `ModelRegistry::update()` read the index under the lock, threw the read error away and wrote the mutated list back — so an unreadable or version-mismatched index meant that every model except the one being registered silently left the registry. Now the index is read with the same lock that guards the write, the version is checked (the loader checks it too), and a broken index is rebuilt from the models directory — the same recovery `load()` does — before the mutation is applied. The caller is told that a rebuild happened (`update(..., bool *rebuilt)`). The models on disk are the truth; the index is the cache of them. Tests: `TestModelRegistry::updateKeepsTheModelsOfABrokenIndex`, `::updateKeepsTheModelsOfAVersionMismatch`. |

| 199  | **The page index is clamped to the document, not to the manifest.** Opening a project whose embedded files are all missing leaves the document empty; the old code clamped the page number to `pageCount() - 1` = -1, which is undefined behaviour (`std::clamp` requires `!(hi < lo)`) and left the page model and the controller disagreeing about the current page. The upper bound comes from the document (`max(0, pageCount() - 1)`), so the index stays at 0. Test: `TestAppImport::projectWithoutEmbeddedFilesOpensToPageZero`. |

| 200  | **The draft of the models table is a set of entries with real properties, and a re-sync keeps the unsaved edits.** The draft used to be a plain JS object that `syncDraft()` rebuilt from the saved settings and assigned as a whole: the rows were bound to a snapshot, and an edit the user had not saved yet (a model name in the external mode, a pick in the wizard step) was thrown away the moment the window or the step was shown again. The draft is now `list<QtObject>` — one entry per task, each with `key`, `quantId`, `modelName` and an «edited» flag per field. `syncDraft()` updates the entries **in place** and refreshes only the fields the user has not touched; `resetDraft()` (the Cancel button) throws the edits away and takes the saved values; `markDraftSaved()` clears the flags after a save. The rows bind to the entry's properties, so a change is a change of the entry and the manual `draftChanged()` is gone. Tests: `TestTaskModelsTableQml::anUnsavedModelNameSurvivesTheSync`, `::theCancelPathThrowsTheEditAway`, `::theTableLoadsWithoutWarnings`. |

| 201  | **One owner of Ctrl+Return.** The block panel and the version card both answered Ctrl+Return, and the panel's condition knew only the page text editor — so the card's own shortcut fired the panel's «approve the block» while a text was being typed in. The card owns the key while it is being edited, and the panel yields: its shortcut is enabled only when no card is editing and the page editor is not focused. Exactly one of the two is active at any moment. |

| 202  | **A failed update check says so.** The release catalog falls back to its cache when the network fails, and the installer reported that as a successful check: the status said «Latest release: bNNNN» and the cache timestamp was refreshed, so the six-hour cache looked fresh. Now the catalog that comes with an error is announced as the cache — «Showing the cached release list — the check failed: …» — the timestamp of the cache is left alone, and the check is retried on the next run. |

| 203  | **The release box follows the installer.** The combo box of the runtime tab took the first release of the list, whatever the installer had selected; the selection is now read from the installer (`syncReleaseBox()`), and the box follows it when the catalog or the selection changes. |

| 204  | **The build turns the warnings on.** The project compiles with `-Wall -Wextra` (`/W4` on MSVC, with the three warnings every Qt header triggers silenced). What surfaced was fixed: the base class was initialized after the members in three constructors, a dead request-dump helper and a dead `ModelProfiles::promptsFor` are gone, an unused parameter of the preset loader now reports the broken user catalog instead of swallowing it, the aggregate initializers name the fields they leave out, and the unused captures are gone. A missed `switch` case or a signed/unsigned mix-up must not pass silently — which is also what makes the ten role switches of the settings store visible (the finding D-020 of the review). |

| 205  | **Three orphan QML files are gone.** `Common/ModelDownloadList.qml`, `Common/InstallModelDialog.qml` and `Common/SplitToolButton.qml` were not used by anything: the models list lives in `Common/TaskModelsTable.qml` and the runtime builds list in `Common/RuntimeBuildsList.qml`. They were deleted, together with their translation contexts. ADR 122 and ADR 158 describe the row pattern of the models list and the shared popups; the component they name no longer exists, and the pattern lives on in the table that replaced it. |
